Rule Algebras for Adhesive Categories \rsuper * Thanks: The work of N.B. was supported by a Marie Skłodowska-Curie Individual Fellowship (Grant Agreement No. 753750 — RaSiR). Thanks: P.S. was supported by the ESF funded Estonian IT Academy research measure (project 2014-2020.4.05.19-0001).
Nicolas Behr \rsuper a, Paweł Sobociński \rsuper b

Abstract
. We demonstrate that the most well-known approach to rewriting graphical structures, the Double-Pushout (DPO) approach, possesses a notion of sequential compositions of rules along an overlap that is associative in a natural sense. Notably, our results hold in the general setting of ℳ \mathcal{M} -adhesive categories. This observation complements the classical Concurrency Theorem of DPO rewriting. We then proceed to define rule algebras in both settings, where the most general categories permissible are the finitary (or finitary restrictions of) ℳ \mathcal{M} -adhesive categories with ℳ \mathcal{M} -effective unions. If in addition a given such category possess an ℳ \mathcal{M} -initial object, the resulting rule algebra is unital (in addition to being associative). We demonstrate that in this setting a canonical representation of the rule algebras is obtainable, which opens the possibility of applying the concept to define and compute the evolution of statistical moments of observables in stochastic DPO rewriting systems.

\lsuper

*A short version of this work has appeared in the Proceedings of the 27th EACSL Annual Conference on Computer Science Logic, CSL 2018
[
9
]

lmcs-doi:
Volume 16, Issue 3, Paper 2

Introduction

Double pushout graph (DPO) rewriting
[
20
]
is the most well-known and influential approach to algebraic graph transformation. The rewriting mechanics are specified in terms of the universal properties of pushouts—for this reason, the approach is domain-independent and instantiates across a number of concrete notions of graphs and graph-like structures. Moreover, the introduction of adhesive, quasi-adhesive, weak adhesive and
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive categories
[
26
,
21
,
19
]
—which, roughly speaking, ensure that the pushouts involved are “well-behaved”, i.e. they satisfy similar exactness properties as pushouts in the category of sets and functions—entails that a standard corpus of theorems
[
31
]
that ensures the “good behaviour” of DPO rewriting holds if the underlying ambient category is (quasi-, weak,
\mathcal{M}
-)adhesive.

An important classical theorem of DPO rewriting is the
Concurrency Theorem

[
16
]
, which involves an analysis of
two
DPO productions applied in series. Given a
dependency relation
(which, intuitively, determines how the right-hand side of the first rule overlaps with the left-hand side of the second), a purely category-theoretic construction results in a
composite
rule which applies the two rules simultaneously. The Concurrency Theorem then states that the two rules can be applied in series in a way consistent with the relevant dependency relation if and only if the composite rule can be applied, yielding the same result.

The operation that takes two rules together with a dependency relation and produces a composite rule
can be considered as an algebraic operation on the set of DPO productions for a given category.
From this viewpoint, it is natural to ask whether this composition
operation is associative. It is remarkable that this appears to have been open until recently: an elementary proof of this, in the context of adhesive categories, was announced by us in the conference version
[
9
]
of this article.

In this extended version we:

\triangleright

generalise the associativity result to the setting of various notions of
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive categories, giving a careful account of the precise technical conditions that are involved in the proof, which is given in its entirety here for the first time;

\triangleright

tie the proof of associativity to the classical Concurrency Theorem
[
16
]
, showing the relevant categorical constructions that are shared by the two results

\triangleright

give a more complete and detailed account of how the associativity theorem leads to the rule algebra framework, on which we elaborate below.

Indeed, associativity is advantageous for a number of reasons. In
[
4
,
6
]
, the first author and his team developed the
rule algebra
framework for a concrete notion of multigraphs. Inspired by a standard construction in mathematical physics, the operation of rule composition along a common interface yields an associative algebra: given a free vector space with basis the set of DPO rules, the product of the associative algebra takes two basis elements to a formal sum, over all possible dependency relations, of their compositions. This associative algebra is useful in applications, being the formal carrier of combinatorial information that underlies
stochastic
interpretations of rewriting. The most famous example in mathematical physics is the Heisenberg-Weyl algebra
[
11
,
12
]
, which served as the starting point for
[
4
]
. Indeed,
[
4
,
6
]
generalised the Heisenberg-Weyl construction from mere set rewriting to multigraph rewriting. Our work, since it is expressed abstractly in terms of
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive categories, entails that the Heisenberg-Weyl and the DPO graph rewriting rule algebra can
both
be seen as two instances of the same construction, expressed in abstract categorical terms.

Structure of the paper.
The necessary categorical preliminaries are collected in Section
1
. Our main original results are collected
in Section
2
: following a brief recap of the DPO framework we first return to the classic Concurrency Theorem in Section
2.1
, then prove our main associativity result (Theorem
8
) in Section
2.2
. We devote Section
3
to developing the rule algebra framework in the abstract setting, and proceed to give a number of applications: Heisenberg-Weyl algebra in Section
3.1
, applications to combinatorics in Section
3.2
and stochastic mechanics in Section
3.3
. Our concluding remarks are in Section
4
.

1.
Background:
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive categories

[

]
(see
[

,

]
for further references).

{defi}

[Van Kampen (VK) squares
[
26
]
]
In a category
\mathbf{C}
, a pushout square
\star
(below left) is a
Van Kampen (VK) square
whenever the following
VK condition
holds: in every commutative cube over the pushout square such as the one below right in which the back and right faces are pullbacks, the top face is a pushout if and only if the front and left faces are pullbacks.

As an important variant (cf. e.g.
[
22
]
), given a class of morphisms
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathsf{mor}(\mathbf{C})
, we let a
weak horizontal (weak vertical)
\mathcal{M}
-VK square
be defined as a pushout square
\star
where the VK condition is only required to hold for those commutative cubes where all horizontal (all vertical) morphisms are in
\mathcal{M}
. Vertical
\mathcal{M}
-VK squares are alternatively referred to as
\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}
-VK squares
.

Various notions of adhesive categories of importance to rewriting theories are known in the literature, with a number of different naming conventions. We opt here to follow the traditional convention
[
18
]
.

{defi}
[Variants of adhesive categories]
Let
\mathbf{C}
be a category.

\triangleright

\mathbf{C}
is an
adhesive category

[
26
]
if

(1)

\mathbf{C}
has pullbacks along arbitrary morphisms,

(2)

\mathbf{C}
has pushouts along monomorphisms, and

(3)

pushouts along monomorphisms are VK squares.

\triangleright

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathsf{mono}(\mathbf{C})
be a class of monomorphisms.

\triangleright

(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
is an
adhesive HLR category

[
16
,
18
]
if

(1)

\mathbf{C}
has pullbacks along
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms,

(2)

\mathbf{C}
has pushouts along
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms,

(3)

pushouts along
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms are VK squares, and

(4)

\mathcal{M}
contains all isomorphisms and is stable under composition, pullback and pushout.

\triangleright

If instead of axiom (III’) above pushouts along
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms are only required to be horizontal VK squares (vertical weak VK squares),
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
is referred to
[
22
]
as a
horizontal weak (vertical weak) adhesive HLR category
. If
\mathcal{M}
-pushouts are both horizontal
and
vertical VK squares,
\mathbf{C}
is called
weak adhesive HLR category
.

As proposed in
[
18
]
, we will alternatively refer to vertical weak adhesive HLR categories simply as
\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}
-adhesive categories
.

{exa}

In order to illustrate the utility of the various different notions of adhesive categories, we list in Table
1
examples for each of these types quoted from the rewriting literature. The table lists additional properties of relevance to the present paper that will be subsequently introduced below. The construction principles used for many of the more sophisticated examples listed in the table are rooted in notions of slice, coslice, functor and comma categories (cf. e.g.
[
16
,
13
]
for further details). As an example of a comma category construction, we introduce in Section
3.2
the category
\mathbf{uGraph}
of finite undirected multigraphs.

Remark 1
.

We would like to emphasise the hierarchy among concepts as implied by Definition
1
, in that every adhesive category is an adhesive HLR category (for
\mathcal{M}=\mathsf{mono}(\mathbf{C})
), while every adhesive HLR category is a weak adhesive HLR category. By definition, weak adhesive HLR categories are both horizontal and vertical weak adhesive HLR categories. As discussed in detail in
[
18
]
, horizontal weak adhesive HLR categories lack certain properties relevant to rewriting, in contrast to the vertical weak variant, which is why the latter is typically considered as the most general type of rewriting-compatible category. Henceforth, we will thus follow the traditional convention to refer to a vertical weak adhesive category as an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category.

In many applications of interest, the data structures to be rewritten satisfy a certain notion of finiteness:

{defi}

[Finitary
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive categories, cf.
[
13
, Defs. 2.8 and 4.1]
]
Let
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
be an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category.
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
is called
finitary
if each object is
finite
(i.e. has only finitely many
\mathcal{M}
-subobjects). The
finitary restriction

(\mathbf{C}_{{\rm fin}},\mathcal{M}_{{\rm fin}})
of
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
is defined as the restriction of
\mathbf{C}
to the full subcategory
\mathbf{C}_{{\rm fin}}
of finite objects, and with
\mathcal{M}_{{\rm fin}}:=\mathcal{M}\cap\mathbf{C}_{{\rm fin}}
.

The following result guarantees that every
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category will give rise to a finitary variant via a form of restriction (i.e. which in particular preserves the adhesivity properties).

{thmC}

[
[
13
, Thm. 4.6]
]
The finitary restriction
(\mathbf{C}_{{\rm fin}},\mathcal{M}_{{\rm fin}})
of an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
is a finitary
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category.

An important concept used throughout the paper is that of (isomorphism classes of) spans of
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms. A span consists of two
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms with a common source
C\xhookleftarrow{b}B\xhookrightarrow{a}A
. A homomorphism of spans from

C\xhookleftarrow{b}B\xhookrightarrow{a}A
to
C\xhookleftarrow{b^{\prime}}B^{\prime}\xhookrightarrow{a^{\prime}}A
consists of a morphism
h\mathrel{:}B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
such
that the two resulting triangles commute. The spans are said to be isomorphic when
h
is an isomorphism. The following shows that isomorphism classes of spans are composable, and so are the arrows of a category
\mathsf{Span}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{C})

with the same objects as
\mathbf{C}
.

Lemma 2
.

Let
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
be an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category, and let
R=(C\xhookleftarrow{b}B\xhookrightarrow{a}A)
and
S=(E\xhookleftarrow{d}D\xhookrightarrow{c}C)
be two composable spans with
a,b,c,d\in\mathcal{M}
. Then their
composition

S\circ R
, calculated via taking the pullback marked
\mathsf{PB}
below,

\hbox to198.56pt{\vbox to67.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 99.27924pt\lower-33.68497pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(1)

is also a span of
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms (i.e.
d\circ f,a\circ e\in\mathcal{M}
).

Proof 1.1
.

The proof follows from the
\mathcal{M}
-adhesivity properties, i.e. from stability of the class
\mathcal{M}
under pullback and composition.

Note in particular that the pullback composition operation
\circ
on spans behaves in complete analogy to the composition operations of functions as well as on linear operators, at least if considering the following convention
1
1

1

This convention is standard in much of the mathematics literature; however, traditionally the opposite convention of reading spans “left-to-right” is encountered in the literature on graph rewriting. Since in our framework we will eventually assign linear operators to spans, the “right-to-left” convention offers the more convenient encoding.
:

{conv}

We read spans in the
“right-to-left” convention
, such that if we consider spans
R,S
as above to encode partial functions
r\mathrel{:}A\rightharpoonup C,\,s\mathrel{:}C\rightharpoonup E
, then function composition and span composition are compatible (i.e.
s\circ r
is computed via
S\circ R
).

1.1.
Some useful technical results

We recall first some basic pasting properties of pushouts and pullbacks that hold in any category.

Lemma 3
.

Given a commutative diagram

(2)

it holds that

(1)

the square marked
(A)
is a pushout for arbitrary morphisms
f
,

(2)

the square marked
(B)
is a pullback if and only if the morphism
g
is a monomorphism

(3)

the square marked
(C)
is a pullback for arbitrary morphisms
f
if
g
is a monomorphism.

In addition, if the category is an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category, statements (I), (III) and the “if” part of (II) hold for “monomorphisms” replaced by “
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms”.

Proof 1.2
.

The statements
(I)
and
(II)

1.2.
Additional category-theoretical prerequisites

Passing from adhesive categories to
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive categories on the one hand permits to study rewriting in the most general setting for DPO rewriting known to date, yet it comes at the price of a number of technicalities that are necessary to ensure certain associativity properties for the rewriting as introduced in the main part of the paper. The first such property concerns the existence of the analogue of the empty set in the category
\mathbf{Set}
or the empty graph in the category
\mathbf{Graph}
, referred to as
\mathcal{M}
-initial object
for a general
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category (the existence of which is not guaranteed by
\mathcal{M}
-adhesivity, cf. Table
1
). The second requirement concerns the property of an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category possessing
\mathcal{M}
-effective unions
, analogous (to a certain extent) to the notion of union of sets and related properties.

{defi}

[
\mathcal{M}
-initial object;
[
13
, Def. 2.5]
]
An object
\mathbb{I}
of an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
is defined to be an
\mathcal{M}
-initial object
if for each object
A\in\mathsf{obj}(\mathbf{C})
there exists a unique monomorphism
i_{A}:\mathbb{I}\hookrightarrow A
, which is moreover required to be in
\mathcal{M}
.

{lemC}

[
[
13
, Fact 2.6]
]
If an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
possesses an
\mathcal{M}
-initial object
\mathbb{I}\in\mathsf{obj}(\mathbf{C})
, the category has
finite coproducts
, and the coproduct injections are in
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof 1.3
.

We quote the proof from
[
13
]
for illustration of this important property: it suffices to consider the case of binary coproducts. One may construct the coproduct
A+B
of two objects
A,B\in\mathsf{obj}(\mathbf{C})
via taking the pushout

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.85}{0.0}{0.0}{0.85}{-63.64919pt}{-34.06274pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

(5)

Since the underlying category is assumed to be
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive, according to Definition
1
the above pushout is guaranteed to exist since
i_{A},i_{B}\in\mathcal{M}
via the assumption of
\mathbb{I}
being an
\mathcal{M}
-initial object, and by virtue of stability of
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms under pushouts, we may moreover conclude that indeed
in_{A},in_{B}\in\mathcal{M}
.

The second main property required for our construction of rule algebras concerns a certain compatibility property relating pushouts and pullbacks along
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms:

{defi}
[
\mathcal{M}
-effective unions]
An
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
is said to possess
\mathcal{M}
-effective unions
if the following property holds: given a commutative diagram as below with
b_{1},b_{2},c_{1},c_{2},d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
, where
(1)
is a pushout, the outer square a pullback, and where
x
is the unique morphism induced by the universal property of the pushout,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

(6)

then
x\in\mathcal{M}
.

As indicated in Table
1
, while the property of
\mathcal{M}
-effective unions is traditionally well-known in a range of important examples, it is nonetheless in general a difficult task to establish this property. Many of the positive examples may be derived via the following (variant of a) result of
[
26
]
:

Theorem 5

(Variant of
[
26
, Thm. 5.1]
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
be a horizontal weak adhesive HLR category. Then in a commutative diagram of the form (
6
),
x\in\mathsf{mono}(\mathbf{C})
is a monomorphism
(not necessarily in
\mathcal{M}
if
\mathcal{M}\neq\mathsf{mono}(\mathbf{C})
)
.

Proof 1.4
.

It may be easily verified that the proof provided in
[
26
]
in the setting of adhesive category in fact utilises only the axioms of horizontal weak adhesive HLR categories, and thus directly generalises to this setting.

As the inspection of Table
1
reveals,
\mathbf{lSet}
is the only known example in which the horizontal, but not also the vertical weak adhesive HLR properties hold, while simultaneously in this category
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{lSet}}\neq\mathsf{mono}(\mathbf{lSet})
. Thus Theorem
5
effectively identifies weak adhesive HLR categories with
\mathcal{M}=\mathsf{mono}(\mathbf{C})
as natural examples of categories with (
\mathsf{mono}(\mathbf{C})
)-effective unions. This includes the original statement that all adhesive categories have effective unions, but also covers examples of (weak) adhesive HLR categories such as
\mathbf{HyperGraph}
and
\mathbf{uGraph}
. As recently demonstrated in
[
22
]
, for
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive categories such as
\mathbf{AGraph}_{\Sigma}
or
\mathbf{SymbGraph}_{D}
where the class
\mathcal{M}
of monomorphisms has additional structure, it is possible to use the statement of Theorem
5
in order to manually prove the existence of
\mathcal{M}
-effective unions. On the other hand, the latter two categories fail to possess an
\mathcal{M}
-initial object, which prevents them from satisfying all assumptions necessary in order to support a unital rule algebra construction (albeit they
do
support the concurrency and associativty property of DPO rule compositions). We leave a more in-depth investigation into these matters of admissibility for future work.

2.
Double-pushout (DPO) rewriting

We now recall
Double-Pushout (DPO) rewriting
for
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive categories (adapted according to the results of
[
13
]
and to our
notational convention

1
).

{asm}

Throughout the remainder of this paper, we fix in each definition an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category

(\mathbf{C},\mathcal{M})
(typically just written as
\mathbf{C}
for brevity) that is assumed to possess an
\mathcal{M}
-initial object and
\mathcal{M}
-effective unions.

{defiC}

[
[
26
, Def. 7.1]
]
A span
p
of morphisms (with
O
utput,
K
ontext,
I
nput)

O\xleftarrow{o}K\xrightarrow{i}I

(7)

is called a
production
.
p
is said to be
linear
if both
i
and
o
are monomorphisms in
\mathcal{M}
. We denote the
set of linear productions
by
\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
. We will also frequently make use of the
alternative notation

O\xleftharpoonup{p}I
where
p=(O\xleftarrow{o}K\xrightarrow{i}I)\in\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
.

A
homomorphism of productions

p\rightarrow p^{\prime}
consists of arrows,
O\rightarrow O^{\prime}
,
K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
and
I\rightarrow I^{\prime}
, such that the obvious diagram commutes. A homomorphism is an isomorphism when all of its components are isomorphisms. We do not distinguish between isomorphic productions. Note that the notion of morphism of productions is different than that for general spans.

{defiC}

[
[
26
, Def. 7.2]
]

p

if there exists an object

More concisely, the

holds if there is a

of

From here on, we will focus solely on
linear productions
and matches that are
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms, which entails due to the above statements a number of practical simplifications, and which allows us to simplify also the notations as follows:

{conv}

Unless mentioned otherwise, henceforward
all
arrows are understood to be morphisms of the class
\mathcal{M}
of the underlying
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category
\mathbf{C}
, whence we will use the notation

of “ordinary” arrows (instead of

\mathcal{M}
in all diagrams and formulae.

{defi}

[compare
[
26
, Def. 7.3]
]
Given an object
X\in\mathsf{obj}(\mathbf{C})
and a linear production
p\in\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})

as the set of monomorphisms

in
\mathcal{M}
for which

satisfies the

. As a consequence, there exist objects and morphisms such that in the diagram below both squares are pushouts (where the square marked

We write

for the object “produced” by the above diagram. The process is called

of

Note that by virtue of Lemma

is

. From here on, we will refer to linear productions as

. Next, we recall the concept of

of linear rules.

2.1.
Concurrent composition and concurrency theorem

Given two linear productions
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
and an object

p_{2}
on a produced object
X^{\prime}=p_{1_{m_{1}}}(X)
(for some admissible match
m_{1}
). However, there exists also the interesting possibility to consider first composing the
rules
in a certain sense, and then applying the

to the object

p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{m}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{1}\in\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
for the

of

with

along the admissible match

, defined as

p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{m}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{1}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.9,0.45,0}(O_{21}\xleftarrow{o_{21}}K_{21}\xrightarrow{i_{21}}I_{21})}:=({\color[rgb]{0.9,0.45,0}O_{12}}\xleftarrow{o_{2}^{\prime}}\overline{K}_{2}\xrightarrow{i_{2}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{21}})\circ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{21}}\xleftarrow{o_{1}^{\prime}}\overline{K}_{1}\xrightarrow{i_{1}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.9,0.45,0}I_{21}})\,

).

The following theorem is a refinement of a well-known result from the literature, where the novel feature of our version that will prove quintessential in the following is the specification of the theorem via
admissible matches of linear rules
(rather than the less specific notion of
E
-concurrent derivations as in the work of Ehrig et al.
[
17
]
). In the adhesive category setting, this approach had already been investigated in
[
26
]
. The reason our modification (which hinges on Assumption
2
) provides a strong improvement over the traditional results resides in the fact that in the synthesis step (see below), one is not only led to derive a certain
cospan
encoding the causal interaction of the two sequentially applied rules, but in fact a
span
of
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms that is unique up to isomorphism, and that thus in a certain sense provides a
minimal
encoding of said causal interaction. Besides practical advantages, this result is in particular strictly necessary in order to lift the notion of associativity of sequential compositions, DPO-type rule algebras and canonical representations of DPO-type rule algebras from the adhesive to the
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive setting.

Theorem 6

(DPO-type Concurrency Theorem; modification of
[
17
, Thm. 4.17]
, compare
[
26
, Thm. 7.11]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category satisfying Assumption
2
. Let
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
be two linear rules and
X_{0}\in\mathsf{obj}(\mathbf{C})
an object.

•

Synthesis:
Given a two-step sequence of derivations

X_{2}\xLeftarrow[p_{2},m_{2}]{}X_{1}\xLeftarrow[p_{1},m_{1}]{}X_{0}\,,

with
X_{1}:=p_{1_{m_{1}}}(X_{0})
and
X_{2}:=p_{2_{m_{2}}}(X_{1})
, there exists a composite rule
q=p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{n}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{1}

for a unique
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbf{M}_{p_{2}}(p_{1})
,
and a unique admissible match
n\in\mathsf{M}_{q}(X)
, such that

q_{n}(X)\xLeftarrow[q,n]{}X_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad q_{n}(X_{0})\cong X_{2}\,.

•

Analysis:
Given an admissible match
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbf{M}_{p_{2}}(p_{1})
of
p_{2}
into
p_{1}
and an admissible match
n\in\mathsf{M}_{q}(X)
of the composite
q=p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{n}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{1}
into
X
, there exists a unique pair of admissible matches
m_{1}\in\mathsf{M}_{p_{1}}(X_{0})
and
m_{2}\in\mathsf{M}_{p_{2}}(X_{1})
(with
X_{1}:=p_{1_{m_{1}}}(X_{0})
) such that

X_{2}\xLeftarrow[p_{2},m_{2}]{}X_{1}\xLeftarrow[p_{1},m_{1}]{}X_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad X_{2}\cong q_{n}(X)\,.

Proof 2.1
.

—
Synthesis:
Consider the setting presented in (
14a
). Here, we have obtained the candidate match
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{n}}=(I_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\leftarrow M_{21}\rightarrow}O_{1})
via pulling back the cospan
(I_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\rightarrow}X_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\leftarrow}O_{1})
. Next, we construct
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{21}}
via taking the pushout of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{n}}
, which induces a unique arrow
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{21}\rightarrow}X_{1}
. Crucially, it follows from Assumption
2
that this arrow is in the class
\mathcal{M}
. The diagram in (
14b
) is obtained by taking the pullbacks of the spans
\overline{K}_{i}\rightarrow X_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\leftarrow N_{21}}
(obtaining the objects
K_{i}^{\prime}
, for
i=1,2
), followed by letting
{\color[rgb]{0.9,0.45,0}O_{21}}:=\mathsf{PO}(O_{2}\leftarrow K_{2}\rightarrow K_{2}^{\prime})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9,0.45,0}I_{21}}:=\mathsf{PO}(O_{1}\leftarrow K_{1}\rightarrow K_{1}^{\prime})
. By virtue of pushout-pullback (Lemma
1.1
) and pushout-pushout decomposition (Lemma
3
), respectively, the resulting squares are all pushouts. The final step as depicted in (
14c
) consists in constructing
{\color[rgb]{0.9,0.45,0}K_{21}}=\mathsf{PB}(K_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{21}}\leftarrow K_{1}^{\prime})
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\overline{K}_{21}}=\mathsf{PB}(\overline{K_{2}}\rightarrow X_{1}\leftarrow\overline{K_{1}})
, which by universality of pullbacks induces a unique arrow
{\color[rgb]{0.9,0.45,0}K_{21}\rightarrow\overline{K}_{21}}
. By invoking pullback decomposition (Lemma
1.1
) and the
\mathcal{M}
-van Kampen property (cf. Def.
1
) twice, one may demonstrate that the squares
\Box({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}K_{21}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\overline{K}_{21}},\overline{K}_{i},K_{i}^{\prime})
(for
i=1,2
) are pushouts. Thus the claim follows by invoking pushout pasting according to Lemma
3
twice in order to obtain the pushout squares
\Box({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}K_{21}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\overline{K}_{21}},X_{2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}O_{21}})
and
\Box({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}K_{21}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\overline{K}_{21}},X_{0},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}I_{21}})
.

—
Analysis:
Given the setting as depicted in (
15a
), we may obtain the configuration of (

(for

), the resulting new squares are all pushouts. Next, by constructing
4
4

4

Since the construction is entirely symmetric in this step, we could have equivalently chosen to define
X_{1}=\mathsf{PO}(\overline{K}_{2}\leftarrow K_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{21}})
.

X_{1}=\mathsf{PO}(\overline{K}_{1}\leftarrow K_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{21}})

\Box({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}K_{21}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\overline{K}_{21}},X_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{21}})

2.2.
Concurrent composition and associativity

While the concurrency theorem (Theorem
6
) for DPO rewriting is classical, to the best of our knowledge the following result is new. It states a certain form of associativity for compositions of linear productions.

Theorem 8

(DPO-type associativity theorem)
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category that satisfies Assumption
2
. Then the composition operation
.\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}.
on linear productions of
\mathbf{C}
is
associative
in the following sense: given linear productions
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
, there exists a bijective correspondence
between pairs of admissible matches
(\mathbf{m}_{21},\mathbf{m}_{3(21)})
and
(\mathbf{m}_{32},\mathbf{m}_{(32)1})
such that

p_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{m}_{3(21)}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}\left(p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{m}_{21}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{1}\right)\;\cong\;\left(p_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{m}_{32}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{2}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{m}_{(32)1}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{1}\,.

(16)

Proof 2.2
.

Since DPO derivations are symmetric, it suffices to show one side of the correspondence. Our proof is constructive, demonstrating how, given a pair of admissible matches

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{m}_{21}}

\displaystyle=(O_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\leftarrow M_{21}\rightarrow}I_{1})\in\mathbf{M}_{p_{2}}(p_{1})

(17)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{m}_{3(21)}}

\displaystyle=(O_{3}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\leftarrow M_{3(21)}\rightarrow}I_{21})\in\mathbf{M}_{p_{3}}(p_{21})\,,\qquad p_{21}=p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{m}_{21}}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{1}\,,

one may uniquely (up to isomorphisms) construct from this information a pair of admissible matches

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{m}_{32}}

\displaystyle=(O_{3}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\leftarrow M_{32}\rightarrow}I_{2})\in\mathbf{M}_{p_{3}}(p_{2})

(18)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{m}_{(32)1}}

\displaystyle=(O_{32}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\leftarrow M_{(32)1}\rightarrow}I_{1})\in\mathbf{M}_{p_{32}}(p_{1})\,,\qquad p_{32}=p_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{m}_{32}}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{2}\,,

and such that the property described in (
16
) holds. We begin by forming the composite rule
p_{3(21)}=p_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{m}_{3(21)}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{21}
, which results in the diagram

(19)

if we invoke Corollary
7
to construct the four rightmost squares on the bottom. Constructing the pullback
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}M_{32}}=\mathsf{PB}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}M_{3(21)}\rightarrow}O_{21}\leftarrow O_{2})
(which by universality of pullbacks also leads to an arrow
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}M_{32}\rightarrow}I_{3}
) and forming the three additional vertical squares on the far left in the evident fashion in the diagram below

(20)

allows us to construct
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{32}}=\mathsf{PO}(I_{3}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\leftarrow M_{32}\rightarrow}O_{2})
, which in turn via universality of pushouts uniquely induces an arrow
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{32}\rightarrow N_{3(21)}}
:

(21)

Here, the rightmost three squares on the top are formed in the evident fashion (and are pushouts according to Lemma
4
), while the other arrows of the above diagram are constructed as follows:

\displaystyle K_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{PB}(\overline{K}_{3}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{3(21)}\leftarrow N_{32}})\,,\quad

\displaystyle O_{32}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{PO}(K_{3}^{\prime}\leftarrow K_{3}\rightarrow O_{3})

(22)

\displaystyle K_{2}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{PB}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{32}\rightarrow N_{3(21)}\leftarrow}\overline{K}_{2})\,,\quad

\displaystyle I_{32}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{PO}(K_{2}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow K_{2}\rightarrow I_{2})

Invoking pushout-pullback and pushout-pushout decomposition repeatedly, it may be verified that all squares thus created on the top and in the front are pushout squares. Defining the pullback object
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}M_{(32)1}}=\mathsf{PB}(I_{32}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\rightarrow N_{3(21)}\leftarrow}O_{1})
, thus inducing an arrow
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}M_{21}\rightarrow M_{3(21)}}
,

(23)

it remains to verify that the square
\Box({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}M_{3(21)}},I_{32},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}N_{3(21)}},O_{1})
is not only a pullback, but also a pushout square. To this end, construct the auxiliary diagram depicted in Figure
, where the top, back, bottom and front cubes that are formed via the newly added arrows compared to (
23
) are also drawn separately for clarity, with suitable 3d rotations applied such as to facilitate the application of further steps in the proof based upon the
\mathcal{M}
-van Kampen property
5
5

5

On a philosophical note, it might be worth observing that while sequential compositions of rules are essentially described by two-dimensional commutative diagrams, this final step of the associativity proof appears to have an inherently
three-dimensional
character, in that the properties of the commutative cubes in question delicately rely on each other as described in the proof.
. Note in particular that the four additional new arrows exist due to universality of pullbacks.

Invoking pullback decomposition as well as the
\mathcal{M}
-van Kampen property repeatedly, the new commutative cube on the top (i.e. the one sitting over the two pushout squares
\Box(M_{32},I_{3},N_{32},O_{2})
and
\Box(K_{2},O_{2},N_{32},K_{2}^{\prime\prime})
) and the new commutative cube on the bottom (i.e. the one sitting under the two pushout squares
\Box(M_{3(21)},I_{3},N_{3(21)},O_{(21)})
and
\Box(K_{2}^{\prime},O_{21},N_{3(21)},\overline{K}_{2})
) have pushouts on all of their faces.

As for the new cubes in the front and back, note first that by virtue of Lemma
4
the back left square
\Box(I_{3},I_{3},N_{3(21)},N_{32})
of the front cube is a pullback, while the square
\Box(M_{32},M_{3(21)},O_{21},O_{2})
had been constructed as a pullback in the main part of the proof. Thus invoking pullback decomposition twice, we may conclude that also the squares
\Box(Q,S,\overline{K}_{2},K_{2}^{\prime\prime})
in the front and
\Box(P,R,I_{32},K_{2})
in the back are pullbacks, whence invoking the
\mathcal{M}
-van Kampen twice allows to conclude that the squares
\Box(Q,S,I_{3},I_{3})
in the front left and
\Box(P,R,M_{3(21)},M_{32})
in the back left are pushouts. Moreover, since isomorphisms are stable under pushouts by virtue of Lemma
4
, we may conclude that
Q\cong S
. We collect all of this information into the following diagram:

To prepare the final steps, let us perform the following “splitting” of the above diagram:

We start the construction from the very left: evidently
\Box(I_{3},M_{3(21)},M_{3(21)},I_{3})
is both a pullback and a pushout. Next, construct the pullbacks
P^{\prime}=\mathsf{PB}(M_{3(21)}\rightarrow I_{3}\leftarrow Q)
and
R^{\prime}=\mathsf{PB}(M_{3(21)}^{\prime}\rightarrow I_{3}\leftarrow S)
; by pushout-pullback decomposition, they split the pushout square
\Box(P,Q,I_{3},M_{23})
on the top and
\Box(R,S,I_{3},M_{3(21)})
into two pushout squares each. The latter also implies that
R^{\prime}\cong R
.

Pasting pushouts, we have that
\Box(M_{3(21)}^{\prime},R^{\prime},S,I_{3})
is a pushout, whence by pushout-pushout decomposition so is
\Box(P^{\prime},R^{\prime},S,Q)
(and thus
P^{\prime}\cong R^{\prime}
).

Next, construct the two pushouts
K_{3}^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{PO}(P^{\prime}\leftarrow P\rightarrow K_{2})
in the top and
\overline{K}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathsf{PO}(R^{\prime}\leftarrow R\rightarrow K_{2}^{\prime})
on the bottom (which implies
\overline{K}_{2}^{\prime}\cong K_{2}^{\prime}
). Pushout-pushout decomposition then entails that
\Box(P^{\prime},K_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime},K_{2}^{\prime\prime},Q)
and
\Box(R^{\prime},\overline{K}_{2}^{\prime},\overline{K}_{2},S)
are pushouts, and consequently so is the square
\Box(K_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime},\overline{K}_{2}^{\prime},\overline{K}_{2},K_{2}^{\prime\prime})
.

We repeat the construction of the previous step and construct the pushouts
I_{2}^{\prime}=\mathsf{PO}(K_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leftarrow K_{2}\rightarrow I_{2})
and
N_{21}^{\prime}=\mathsf{PO}(\overline{K}_{2}^{\prime}\leftarrow K_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow N_{21})
(which implies that
N_{21}^{\prime}\cong N_{21}
). Pushout-pushout decomposition then yields the pushout squares
\Box(K_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime},I_{2}^{\prime},I_{32},K_{2}^{\prime\prime})
and
\Box(\overline{K}_{2}^{\prime},N_{21}^{\prime},N_{(32)1},\overline{K}_{2})
, and thus also
\Box(I_{2}^{\prime},N_{21}^{\prime},N_{(32)1},I_{32})
is a pushout.

Next, split the pullback squares
\Box(M_{21},M_{(32)1},I_{32},I_{2})
in the top and

\Box(O_{1},O_{1},N_{(32)1},N_{21})

on the bottom into two pullback squares each via pullback-pullback decomposition, whence via letting
M_{21}^{\prime}=\mathsf{PB}(I_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow I_{32}\leftarrow M_{(32)1})
and
O_{1}^{\prime}=\mathsf{PB}(N_{21}^{\prime}\rightarrow N_{3(21)}\leftarrow O_{1})
. By virtue of Lemma
4
(i.e. stability of isomorphisms under pullbacks), this entails that
O_{1}^{\prime}\cong O_{1}
, whence the square
\Box(O_{1},O_{1}^{\prime},N_{21}^{\prime},N_{21})
is a pushout.

By pullback-pullback decomposition, the square
\Box(M_{21}^{\prime},O_{1}^{\prime},N_{21}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})
is a pullback. By pushout-pullback decomposition, since by virtue of the previous step the square

\Box(M_{21},O_{1}^{\prime},N_{21}^{\prime},I_{2})

is a pushout and
\Box(M_{21}^{\prime},O_{1}^{\prime},N_{21}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})
a pullback,
\Box(M_{21}^{\prime},O_{1}^{\prime},N_{21}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})
is also a pushout.

Finally, since by pushout pasting the square
\Box(M_{21}^{\prime},O_{1}^{\prime},N_{(32)1},I_{32})
is a pushout, and since
\Box(M_{(32)1},O_{1},N_{(32)1},I_{32})
is by construction a pullback, pushout-pullback decomposition entails that
\Box(M_{(32)1},O_{1},N_{(32)1},I_{32})
is also a pushout, which concludes the proof.

In summary, the associativity property manifests itself in the following form, whereby the data provided along the path highlighted in orange below permits to uniquely compute the data provided along the path highlighted in blue (with both sets of overlaps computing the same “triple composite” production):

(24)

3.
From associativity of concurrent derivations to rule algebras

In DPO rewriting, each linear rewriting rule has a non-deterministic effect when acting on a given object, in the sense that there generically exist multiple possible choices of admissible match of the rule into the object. One interesting way of incorporating this non-determinism into a mathematical rewriting framework is motivated by the physics literature:

\triangleright

Each linear rule is lifted to an element of an abstract
vector space
.

\triangleright

Concurrent composition of linear rules is lifted to a
bilinear multiplication operation
on this abstract vector space, endowing it with the structure of an
algebra
.

\triangleright

The action of rules on objects is implemented by mapping each linear rule (seen as an element of the abstract algebra) to an endomorphism on an abstract vector space whose basis vectors are in bijection with the objects of the adhesive category.

While this recipe might seem somewhat ad hoc, we will demonstrate in Section
3.1
that it recovers in fact one of the key constructions of quantum physics and enumerative combinatorics, namely we recover the well-known Heisenberg-Weyl algebra and its canonical representation.

Let us first fix the precise type of categories for which our constructions are well-defined. A very general class of such categories is covered by the following set of assumptions:

{asm}

We assume from hereon that
\mathbf{C}
is a
finitary
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category
with
\mathcal{M}
-effective unions.

Let us next quote some concepts of general mathematics of key relevance to the material presented in this section:

{defi}
[cf. e.g.
[
24
]
]
Let
V\equiv(V,+,\cdot)
be a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
, with
\mathbb{K}
a field (e.g.
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
). Then a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra

(V,*)
is defined via equipping the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
V
with a
bilinear binary operation

*:V\times V\rightarrow V
. Here, bilinearity entails that

\forall a,b\in V\,,\;\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{K}:\quad(\alpha\cdot a)*(\beta\cdot b)=(\alpha\beta)\cdot(a*b)\,.

(25)

The algebra
(V,*)
is called
associative
if

\forall a,b,c\in V:\quad a*(b*c)=(a*b)*c\,.

(26)

It is called
unital
if there exists an element
u\in V
(then referred to as a
unit element
) such that

\forall v\in V:\quad u*v=v*u=v\,.

(27)

Let
W\equiv(W,+,\cdot)
be another
\mathbb{K}
-vector space (over the same field
\mathbb{K}
as the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
V
), and denote by
End_{\mathbb{K}}(W)
the
algebra of endomorphisms over
W
. Then a
representation

\rho:(V,*)\rightarrow End_{\mathbb{K}}(W)

of the associative
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
(V,*)
is defined as an
algebra homomorphism
, such that

\forall a,b\in V:\quad\rho(a*b)=\rho(a)\rho(b)\,.

(28)

If
(V,*)
is in addition unital, we also require that
\rho
maps the unit element
u\in V
to the identity element of
End_{\mathbb{K}}(W)
,

\rho(u)=\mathbb{1}_{End_{\mathbb{K}}(W)}\,.

(29)

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}\equiv(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}},+,\cdot)
be the free
\mathbb{R}
-vector space on
\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
, defined concretely via a bijection
\delta:\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})_{\cong}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}
from
isomorphism classes of linear productions
in
\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
to the set of basis vectors of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}
. In order to distinguish between elements of
\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
and of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}
, we introduce the notation

(O\xLeftarrow{r}I):=\delta\left(O\xleftharpoonup{r}I\right)\,.

(30)

We will later refer to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}
as the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space of
rule algebra elements
.

{defi}

Define the
DPO rule algebra product

*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}
on an
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category
\mathbf{C}
satisfying Assumption
3
as the binary operation

*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}:\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}\times\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}:(R_{1},R_{2})\mapsto R_{1}*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}R_{2}\,,

(31)

where for two basis vectors
R_{i}=\delta(p_{i})
encoding the linear rules
p_{i}\in\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
(
i=1,2
),

R_{2}*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}R_{1}:=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{M}_{p_{2}}(p_{1})}\delta\left(p_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{m}}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{1}\right)\,.

(32)

Here, we take the notational convention that
\sum_{\emptyset}\dotsc=0_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}
(i.e. the summation over an empty set of admissible matches evaluates to the zero element of the vector space
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}
). The definition is extended to arbitrary (finite) linear combinations of basis vectors by bilinearity, whence for
p_{i},p_{j}\in\mathsf{Lin}(\mathbf{C})
and
\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\cdot\delta(p_{i})\right)*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}\left(\sum_{j}\beta_{j}\cdot\delta(p_{j})\right):=\sum_{i,j}(\alpha_{i}\cdot\beta_{j})\cdot\left(\delta(p_{i})*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}\delta(p_{j})\right)\,.

(33)

We refer to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}\equiv(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}},*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}})
as the
rule algebra
(of linear DPO-type rewriting rules over the
\mathcal{M}
-adhesive category
\mathbf{C}
).

It is worthwhile noting that if the category
\mathbf{C}
possesses an
\mathcal{M}
-initial object, the “trivial match” of two linear productions
p_{j}=(O_{j}\leftarrow K_{j}\rightarrow I_{j})
(for
j=1,2
), i.e.
\mathbf{m}_{\mathbb{I}}=(I_{2}\leftarrow\mathbb{I}\rightarrow O_{1})
, may be verified to be always an admissible match according to the definition of the DPO-type concurrent composition of productions (Definition
2.1
) and by virtue of Lemma
1.2
.

Theorem 9
.

For every category
\mathbf{C}
satisfying Assumption
3
, the associated DPO-type rule algebra
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}\equiv(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}},*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}})
is an
associative algebra
. If
\mathbf{C}
in addition possesses an
\mathcal{M}
-initial object

\mathbb{I}\in\mathsf{obj}(\mathbf{C})
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}
is in addition a
unital algebra
, with unit element
R_{\mathbb{I}}:=(\mathbb{I}\xLeftarrow{id_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathbb{I})
.

Proof 3.1
.

Associativity follows immediately from the associativity of the operation
.\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}.
proved in Theorem
8
. The claim that
R_{\mathbb{I}}
is the unit element of the rule algebra
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}
of an adhesive category
\mathbf{C}
with strict initial object follows directly from the definition of the rule algebra product for
R_{\mathbb{I}}*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}R
and
R*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}R_{\mathbb{I}}
for
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}
. For clarity, we present below the category-theoretic composition calculation that underlies the equation
R_{\mathbb{I}}*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{C}}}R=R
:

(37)

3.1.
Recovering the blueprint: the Heisenberg-Weyl algebra

As a first consistency check and interesting special (and arguably simplest) case of rule algebras, consider the adhesive category
\mathbb{F}
of equivalence classes of finite sets, and functions. This category might alternatively be interpreted as the category
\mathbf{G}_{0}
of isomorphism classes of
finite discrete graphs
, whose linear rules are precisely the injective partial morphisms of discrete graphs. Specialising to a subclass or linear rules, namely to those with a trivial context object,

O\xleftharpoonup{\emptyset}I\equiv(O\leftarrow\emptyset\rightarrow I)\,,

we recover the famous Heisenberg-Weyl algebra and its canonical representation.

{defi}

[c.f. e.g.
[
12
]
]
The associative unital
Heisenberg-Weyl algebra
over
\mathbb{R}
is defined as

A_{HW}:=\frac{\mathbb{R}[\pi^{{\dagger}},\pi]}{\langle[\pi,\pi^{{\dagger}}]-id\rangle}\,,

(39)

where
\mathbb{R}[\pi^{{\dagger}},\pi]
denotes the polynomial ring over
\mathbb{R}
with two generators
\pi^{{\dagger}}
and
\pi
(the “creator” and the “annihilator”) quotiented by the ideal generated by the
canonical commutation relation

[\pi,\pi^{{\dagger}}]\equiv\pi\pi^{{\dagger}}-\pi^{{\dagger}}\pi=id\,.

(40)

Two well-known constructions of representations of
A_{HW}
illustrate the important role of this algebra in applications of combinatorics and physics:

\triangleright

The
Bargmann-Fock (BF) representation
is defined as the algebra homomorphism from
A_{HW}
to the space of endomorphisms over
\mathbb{R}[z]
(the vector space of polynomials in the formal variable
z
) that maps
\pi^{{\dagger}}
to the linear operator
\hat{z}
, and
\pi
to
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}
, with

\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:\quad\hat{z}z^{n}:=z^{n+1}\,,\quad\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}z^{0}:=0\,,\quad\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}z^{n}:=nz^{n-1}\;(n>0)\,.

(41)

The canonical commutation relation may then be explicitly verified to hold on each basis vector
z^{n}
:

\displaystyle z^{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\hat{z}z^{0}-\hat{z}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}z^{0}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}z^{1}-0=z^{0}

(42)

\displaystyle[\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z},\hat{z}]z^{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\hat{z}z^{n}-\hat{z}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}z^{0}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}z^{n+1}-n\hat{z}z^{n-1}=z^{n}\;(n>0)

\triangleright

The so-called
canonical representation
of the HW algebra (see (IV) in Theorem
11
), which is manifestly isomorphic to the BF representation via
\hat{z}\leftrightarrow a^{{\dagger}}
,
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\leftrightarrow a
and
z^{n}\leftrightarrow\left|n\right\rangle
, is the formulation typically encountered in the combinatorics and physics literature (the latter especially in the context of quantum mechanics).

We will now provide a realisation of the Heisenberg-Weyl algebra directly within the DPO rule algebra formalism. Reserving the symbol
\mathcal{H}
for this realisation of the algebra to avoid confusion, note in particular that we give a very intuitive and
intrinsic
meaning to the “creator” and the “annihilator” (as simply the rule algebra elements associated to the linear rules of creating and of deleting a vertex, respectively).

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{R}_{0}\equiv\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}
denote the rule algebra of DPO type rewriting for finite discrete graphs. Then the subalgebra
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is defined as the algebra whose elementary generators are

x^{{\dagger}}:=(\bullet\xLeftarrow{\emptyset}\emptyset)\equiv\delta(\bullet\leftarrow\emptyset\rightarrow\emptyset)\,,\quad x:=(\emptyset\xLeftarrow{\emptyset}\bullet)\equiv\delta(\emptyset\leftarrow\emptyset\rightarrow\bullet)\,,

(43)

and whose elements are (finite) linear combinations of words in
x^{{\dagger}}
and
x
(with concatenation given by the rule algebra multiplication
*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
) and of the unit element
R_{\emptyset}=(\emptyset\xLeftarrow{\emptyset}\emptyset)
. The canonical representation of
\mathcal{H}
is the restriction of the canonical representation of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
to
\mathcal{H}
.

The following theorem demonstrates how well-known properties of the Heisenberg-Weyl algebra (see e.g.
[
12
,
6
,
7
]
and references therein) follow directly from the previously introduced constructions of the rule algebra and its canonical representation. This justifies our claim that the Heisenberg-Weyl construction is a special case of our general framework.

Theorem 11

(Heisenberg-Weyl algebra from discrete graph rewriting rule algebra)
.

(1)

For integers
m,n>0
,

\underbrace{x^{{\dagger}}*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\dotsc*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}x^{{\dagger}}}_{\text{$m$ times}}=\underbrace{x^{{\dagger}}\uplus\dotsc\uplus x^{{\dagger}}}_{\text{$m$ times}}\,,\quad\underbrace{x*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\dotsc*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}x}_{\text{$n$ times}}=\underbrace{x\uplus\dotsc\uplus x}_{\text{$n$ times}}\,,

(44)

where we define for linear rules
p_{1},p_{2}\in Lin(\mathbf{C})

\delta(p_{1})\uplus\delta(p_{2}):=\delta(p_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\emptyset}}{{\blacktriangleleft}}p_{2})\,.

(45)

(2)

The generators
x,x^{{\dagger}}\in\mathcal{H}
fulfil the
canonical commutation relation

[x,x^{{\dagger}}]\equiv x*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}x^{{\dagger}}-x^{{\dagger}}*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}x=R_{\emptyset}\,,\quad R_{\emptyset}=(\emptyset\xLeftarrow{\emptyset}\emptyset)\,.

(46)

(3)

Every element of
\mathcal{H}
may be expressed as a (finite) linear combination of so-called
normal-ordered
expressions
x^{{\dagger}\>*r}*x^{*s}
(with
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
*\equiv*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
). By convention,
x^{{\dagger}\>*r}:=x^{{\dagger}}*\dotsc*x^{{\dagger}}
(a product of
r
generators
x^{{\dagger}}
), and analogously for
x^{*\>s}
.

(4)

Denoting by
\left|n\right\rangle\equiv\left|\bullet^{\uplus\>n}\right\rangle
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
) the basis vector associated to the discrete graph with
n
vertices in the vector space
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{0}
of isomorphism classes of discrete graphs, the canonical representation of
\mathcal{H}
according to Definition
3.1
reads explicitly

a^{{\dagger}}\left|n\right\rangle=\left|n+1\right\rangle\,,\quad a\left|n\right\rangle=\begin{cases}n\cdot\left|n-1\right\rangle\quad&\text{if }n>0\\
0_{\hat{G}_{0}}&\text{else}\end{cases}\,,

(47)

with
a^{{\dagger}}:=\rho_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}(x^{{\dagger}})
(the
creation operator
) and
a:=\rho_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}(x)
(the
annihilation operator
).

Proof 3.3
.

(1)

Since there is no partial injection possible between the input of one copy and the output of another copy of
x^{{\dagger}}
other than the trivial match, and similarly for two copies of
x
, the claim follows by induction.

(2)

Computing the commutator
[x,x^{{\dagger}}]=x*x^{{\dagger}}-x^{{\dagger}}*x
(with
*\equiv*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
) explicitly, we find that

x*x^{{\dagger}}=x\uplus x^{{\dagger}}+R_{\emptyset}\,,\quad x^{{\dagger}}*x=x^{{\dagger}}\uplus x\,,

(48)

from which the claim follows due to commutativity of the operation
\uplus
on
\mathcal{R}_{0}
,
x\uplus x^{{\dagger}}=x^{{\dagger}}\uplus x
. Here, the contribution
R_{\emptyset}
arises from the following sequential composition:

(3)

It suffices to prove the statement for basis elements of
\mathcal{H}
. Consider thus an arbitrary composition of a finite number of copies of the generators
x
and
x^{{\dagger}}
. Then by repeated application of the commutation relation
[x,x^{{\dagger}}]=R_{\emptyset}
, and since
R_{\emptyset}
is the unit element for
*_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
, we can convert the arbitrary basis element of
\mathcal{H}
into a linear combination of normal-ordered elements. More explicitly, from the viewpoint of the composition operation on linear rules, an expression of the form
x^{{\dagger}\>*r}*x^{*\>s}
is easily verified to evaluate to

x^{{\dagger}\>*r}*x^{*\>s}=\delta\left(\bullet^{\uplus\>r}\leftarrow\emptyset\rightarrow\bullet^{\uplus\>s}\right)\,.

(50)

In full analogy to the computation presented in (
49
), composing
x^{{\dagger}\>*r}*x^{*\>s}
with
x^{{\dagger}\>*k}*x^{*\>\ell}
will evaluate to

x^{{\dagger}\>*r}*x^{*\>s}*x^{{\dagger}\>*k}*x^{*\>\ell}=\sum_{n=0}^{\min(s,k)}\tfrac{s!k!}{(s-n)!n!(k-n)!}\,x^{{\dagger}\>*(r+k-n)}*x^{*\>(s+\ell-n)}\,.

(51)

Note in particular that the coefficient of a term
x^{{\dagger}\>*(r+k-n)}*x^{*\>(s+\ell-n)}
in the above sum coincides with the number of ways to match
n
of the vertices between a discrete graph with
s
vertices (i.e. the input interface of the rule encoded in
x^{{\dagger}\>*r}*x^{*\>s}
) and a discrete graph with
k
vertices (i.e. the output interface of the rule encoded in
x^{{\dagger}\>*k}*x^{*\>\ell}
). Thus the DPO algebra implementation precisely explains the full combinatorics involved in the problem of “normal-ordering” expressions in the HW algebra.

(4)

Note first that by definition
\left|0\right\rangle=\left|\emptyset\right\rangle
. To prove the claim that for all
n\geq 0

a^{{\dagger}}\left|n\right\rangle=\left|n+1\right\rangle\,,

we apply Definitions
2
and
3.1
by computing the following diagram (compare (
9
)): there exists precisely one admissible match of the empty graph
\emptyset\in\mathsf{obj}(\mathbf{G}_{0})
into the
n
-vertex discrete graph
\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to3.39pt{\vbox to3.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-18.22308pt\lower 18.22308pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\normalsize{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 29.63 27.56 C 29.63 28.7 28.7 29.63 27.56 29.63 C 26.42 29.63 25.49 28.7 25.49 27.56 C 25.49 26.42 26.42 25.49 27.56 25.49 C 28.7 25.49 29.63 26.42 29.63 27.56 Z M 27.56 27.56}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7}{0.0}{0.0}{0.7}{19.91684pt}{19.91684pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}^{\uplus\>n}
, whence constructing the pushout complement marked with dashed arrows and the pushout marked with dotted arrows we verify the claim:

3.2.
Applications of rule algebras to combinatorics

Here we consider an example application, working with undirected multigraphs.

{defi}
[Compare e.g.
[
30
]
]
Let
Id_{\mathbf{Set}}:\mathbf{Set}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
be the identity functor on
\mathbf{Set}
, and let
\mathcal{P}^{(1,2)}:\mathbf{Set}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
be the restricted covariant power set functor (which maps a set
S
to its subsets of cardinality
1
or
2
). Then the
category of finite undirected multigraphs

\mathbf{uGraph}
is defined as the finitary restriction of a comma category
[
16
]
,

\mathbf{uGraph}:={(Id_{\mathbf{Set}},\mathcal{P}^{(1,2)})}_{{\rm fin}}\,.

(52)

An object
U
of
\mathbf{uGraph}
is specified via the data
U\equiv(V_{U},E_{U},inc_{U})
, where
V_{U}
is a set of vertices,
E_{U}
a set of edges, and
inc_{U}:E_{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{(1,2)}(V_{U})
is the incidence function.

Lemma 12
.

\mathbf{uGraph}
is a weak adhesive HLR category, for
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{uGraph}}
the class of pairs of monomorphisms in
\mathbf{Set}_{{\rm fin}}
. It has an
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{uGraph}}
-initial object (the empty graph
\emptyset\in\mathsf{obj}(\mathbf{uGraph})
) as well as
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{uGraph}}
-effective unions.

Proof 3.4
.

The identity functor
Id_{\mathbf{Set}}
trivially preserves pushouts in
\mathbf{Set}
, while
\mathcal{P}^{(1,2)}
preserves pullbacks along monomorphisms in
\mathbf{Set}
(cf. e.g. Appendix A, Cor. 7 of
[
30
]
). Therefore, by
[
16
, Thm. 4.15]
,
(Id_{\mathbf{Set}},\mathcal{P}^{(1,2)})
is a weak adhesive HLR category, and then by Theorem
1
, so is its finitary restriction
\mathbf{uGraph}
.
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{uGraph}}
-initiality is trivial, while the property of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{uGraph}}
-effective unions follows by application of Theorem
5
.

In order to illustrate the intimate interplay of rule algebraic and combinatorial structures, we will now provide an example where the integer coefficients arising in applications of rules of undirected multigraphs yield a known combinatorial integer sequence.

{defi}

We define the algebra
\mathcal{A}
as the one generated
6
6

6

As in the case of the Heisenberg-Weyl algebra, by “generated” we understand that a generic element of
\mathcal{A}
is a finite linear combination of (finite) words in the generators and of the identity element
R_{\emptyset}
, with concatenation given by the rule algebra composition.

(53)

For convenience, we adopt here a graphical notation (so-called “rule diagrams”
[
4
]
) in which we depict a rule algebra basis element
(O\xLeftarrow{f}I)\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{uGraph}}
as the graph of its induced injective partial morphism
(I\xrightharpoonup{f}O)\in Inj(I,O)
of graphs
I
and
O
, with the input graph
I
drawn at the
bottom
, the output graph
O
at the
top
, and where the structure of the morphism
f
is indicated with
dotted lines
. In the example above,
e_{+}
encodes (up to the factor of
\tfrac{1}{2}
chosen purely for convenience) a linear rule with input interface given by two vertices; in applying the rule, the vertices are to be kept (indicated by the vertical dotted lines), while a new edge between them is created (indicated by the
\times
and dotted line to the edge, symbolising “creation”). Dually,
e_{-}
encodes the deletion of an edge, while
d
encodes an identity rule on the pattern of two disjoint vertices. In all three rules, the factor
\tfrac{1}{2}
is chosen such as to compensate for the symmetry of the three linear rules evident from the depictions (i.e. along the horizontal mirror axis of the “rule diagrams”).

The algebra thus defined may be characterised
7
7

7

“Characterised” here refers to the observation that by utilising the commutation relations given in (
54
), it is possible to express an arbitrary element of the algebra as a linear combination in “normal-ordered terms”
e_{+}^{*\>p}*e_{-}^{*\>m}*d^{*\>n}
(for
p,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
).
via its
commutation relations
, which read (with
[x,y]:=x*y-y*x
for
*\equiv*_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{uGraph}}}
)

[e_{-},e_{+}]=d\,,\quad[e_{+},d]=[e_{-},d]=0\,.

(54)

Here, the only nontrivial contribution (i.e. the one that renders the first commutator non-zero) may be computed from the DPO-type composition diagram
8
8

8

Note that the number indices are used solely to specify the precise structure of the match, and are not to be understood as actual vertex labels or types.

We find an interesting structure for the representation of
\mathcal{A}
:

Lemma 13
.

Let
E_{\pm}:=\rho(e_{\pm})
and
D:=\rho(d)
(for
\rho\equiv\rho_{\mathbf{uGraph}}
), and let
\hat{\mathbf{G}}:=\widehat{\mathbf{uGraph}}
. Denote for each non-negative integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{n}\subset\hat{\mathbf{G}}
the linear subspace of
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
spanned by basis vectors indexed by isomorphism classes of undirected graphs with
n
vertices. Then the linear endomorphisms
\rho(X)
for
X\in\{e_{+},e_{-},d\}
possess the vector spaces
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{n}
as
invariant subspaces
, resulting in the
decompositions

\rho(X)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\left(\rho(X)|_{\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{n}}\right)\,.

(56)

Proof 3.5
.

The three rules that define the algebra
\mathcal{A}
do not modify the number of vertices when applied to a given graph (via the canonical representation). In other words, for each
X\in\{e_{+},e_{-},d\}
and for a basis vector
\left|G\right\rangle
of the invariant subspace
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{n}
of graphs with
n
vertices, the image of
\left|G\right\rangle
under application of
\rho(X)
is a linear combination of basis vectors again in
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{n}
, i.e.
\rho(X)\left|G\right\rangle\subset\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{n}
.

Remark 14
.

While at first a rather technical observation, the decomposition of the linear operators
E_{\pm}=\rho(e_{\pm})
and
D=\rho(d)
via restriction to their invariant subspaces gives rise to rich combinatorial structures, even though the operators
E_{\pm}
and
D
originate from representations of very simple rule algebra elements. Since a subspace
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{n}
is characterised by isomorphism classes of finite undirected multigraphs with a fixed number
n
of vertices, but with an arbitrary (finite) number of edges, each space
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{n}
is countably infinite-dimensional. We will exemplify the combinatorial structures that arise for the cases
n=2
and
n=3
in the following.

One may easily verify that the operator
D
may be equivalently expressed as

D=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\rho\left(\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to20.6pt{\vbox to28.57pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.33308pt\lower-22.24991pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{ {{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -22.05}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\hss} {{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 22.05 -22.05}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\right)\,.

(57)

Since the diagonal operator
O_{\bullet}
when applied to an arbitrary graph state
\left|G\right\rangle
for
G\in\mathbf{G}
effectively counts the number
n_{V}(G)
of vertices of
G
,

O_{\bullet}\left|G\right\rangle=n_{V}(G)\left|G\right\rangle\,,

(58)

one finds that

D\left|G\right\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2}O_{\bullet}(O_{\bullet}-1)\left|G\right\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2}n_{V}(G)(n_{V}(G)-1)\left|G\right\rangle\,.

(59)

One may thus alternatively analyse the canonical representation of
\mathcal{A}
split into invariant subspaces of
D
. The lowest non-trivial such subspace is the space
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{2}
of undirected multigraphs on two vertices. It in fact contains a representation of the Heisenberg-Weyl algebra, with
E_{+}
and
E_{-}
taking the roles of the creation and of the annihilation operator, respectively, and with the number vectors
\left|n\right\rangle\equiv\left|\bullet^{\uplus\>n}\right\rangle
implemented as follows (with
{(m)}_{n}:=\Theta(m-n)m!/(m-n)!
):
